See the License for the\nspecific language governing permissions and limitations under the License.\n\n⚠️ Note that this file is in Markdown but contains specific syntax for our doc-builder (similar to MDX) that may not be\nrendered properly in your Markdown viewer.\n\n-->\n\n# Caching\n\nImagine you're having a conversation with someone, and instead of remembering what they previously said, they have to start from scratch every time you respond. This would be slow and inefficient, right?\n\nYou can extend this analogy to transformer models. Autoregressive model generation can be slow because it makes a prediction one token at a time. Each new prediction is dependent on all the previous context.\n\nTo predict the 1000th token, the model requires information from the previous 999 tokens. The information is represented as matrix multiplications across the token representations.\n\nTo predict the 1001th token, you need the same information from the previous 999 tokens in addition to any information from the 1000th token. This is a lot of matrix multiplications a model has to compute over and over for each token!\n\nA key-value (KV) cache eliminates this inefficiency by storing kv pairs derived from the attention layers of previously processed tokens. The stored kv pairs are retrieved from the cache and reused for subsequent tokens, avoiding the need to recompute.\n\n> [!WARNING]\n> Caching should only be used for **inference**. It may cause unexpected errors if it's enabled during training.\n\nTo better understand how and why caching works, let's take a closer look at the structure of the attention matrices.\n\n## Attention matrices\n\nThe **scaled dot-product attention** is calculated as shown below for a batch of size `b`, number of attention heads `h`, sequence length so far `T`, and dimension per attention head `d_head`.\n\n$$\n\\text{Attention}(Q, K, V) = \\text{softmax}\\left( \\frac{Q K^\\top}{\\sqrt{d_{\\text{head}}}} \\times \\text{mask} \\right) V\n$$\n\nThe query (`Q`), key (`K`), and value (`V`) matrices are projections from the input embeddings of shape `(b, h, T, d_head)`.\n\nFor causal attention, the mask prevents the model from attending to future tokens. Once a token is processed, its representation never changes with respect to future tokens, which means $ K_{\\text{past}} $ and $ V_{\\text{past}} $ can be cached and reused to compute the last token's representation.\n\n$$\n\\text{Attention}(q_t, [\\underbrace{k_1, k_2, \\dots, k_{t-1}}_{\\text{cached}}, k_{t}], [\\underbrace{v_1, v_2, \\dots, v_{t-1}}_{\\text{cached}}, v_{t}])\n$$\n\nAt inference time, you only need the last token's query to compute the representation $ x_t $ that predicts the next token $ t+1 $. At each step, the new key and value vectors are **stored** in the cache and **appended** to the past keys and values.\n\n$$\nK_{\\text{cache}} \\leftarrow \\text{concat}(K_{\\text{past}}, k_t), \\quad V_{\\text{cache}} \\leftarrow \\text{concat}(V_{\\text{past}}, v_t)\n$$\n\nAttention is calculated independently in each layer of the model, and caching is done on a per-layer basis.\n\nRefer to the table below to compare how caching improves efficiency.\n\n| without caching | with caching |\n|---|---|\n| for each step, recompute all previous `K` and `V`  | for each step, only compute current `K` and `V` |\n| attention cost per step is **quadratic** with sequence length | attention cost per step is **linear** with sequence length (memory grows linearly, but compute/token remains low) |\n\n## Cache class\n\nA basic KV cache interface takes a key and value tensor for the current token and returns the updated `K` and `V` tensors. This is internally managed by a model's `forward` method.\n\n```py\nnew_K, new_V = cache.update(k_t, v_t, layer_idx)\nattn_output = attn_layer_idx_fn(q_t, new_K, new_V)\n```\n\nWhen you use Transformers' [`Cache`] class, the self-attention module performs several critical steps to integrate past and present information.\n\n1. The attention module concatenates current kv pairs with past kv pairs stored in the cache. This creates attentions weights with the shape `(new_tokens_length, past_kv_length + new_tokens_length)`. The current and past kv pairs are essentially combined to compute the attention scores, ensuring a model is aware of previous context and the current input.\n\n2. When the `forward` method is called iteratively, it's crucial that the attention mask shape matches the combined length of the past and current kv pairs. The attention mask should have the shape `(batch_size, past_kv_length + new_tokens_length)`. This is typically handled internally in [`~GenerationMixin.generate`], but if you want to implement your own generation loop with [`Cache`], keep this in mind! The attention mask should hold the past and current token values.\n\n## Cache storage implementation\n\nCaches are structured as a list of layers, where each layer contains a key and value cache. The key and value caches are tensors with the shape `[batch_size, num_heads, seq_len, head_dim]`.\n\nLayers can be of different types (e.g. `DynamicLayer`, `StaticLayer`, `StaticSlidingWindowLayer`), which mostly changes how sequence length is handled and how the cache is updated.\n\nThe simplest is a `DynamicLayer` that grows as more tokens are processed. The sequence length dimension (`seq_len`) increases with each new token:\n\n```py\ncache.layers[idx].keys = torch.cat([cache.layers[idx].keys, key_states], dim=-2)\ncache.layers[idx].values = torch.cat([cache.layers[idx].values, value_states], dim=-2)\n```\n\nOther layer types like `StaticLayer` and `StaticSlidingWindowLayer` have a fixed sequence length that is set when the cache is created. This makes them compatible with `torch.compile`. In the case of `StaticSlidingWindowLayer`, existing tokens are shifted out of the cache when a new token is added.\n\nThe example below demonstrates how to create a generation loop with [`DynamicCache`]. As discussed, the attention mask is a concatenation of past and current token values.\n\n```py\nimport torch\nfrom transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM, DynamicCache\nfrom accelerate import Accelerator\n\ndevice = Accelerator().device\n\nmodel_id = \"meta-llama/Llama-2-7b-chat-hf\"\nmodel = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(model_id, dtype=torch.bfloat16, device_map=device)\ntokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_id)\n\npast_key_values = DynamicCache(config=model.config)\nmessages = [{\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"Hello, what's your name.\"}]\ninputs = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages, add_generation_prompt=True, return_tensors=\"pt\", return_dict=True).to(model.device)\n\ngenerated_ids = inputs.input_ids\nmax_new_tokens = 10\n\nfor _ in range(max_new_tokens):\n    outputs = model(**inputs, past_key_values=past_key_values, use_cache=True)\n    # Greedily sample one next token\n    next_token_ids = outputs.logits[:, -1:].argmax(-1)\n    generated_ids = torch.cat([generated_ids, next_token_ids], dim=-1)\n    # Prepare inputs for the next generation step by leaving unprocessed tokens, in our case we have only one new token\n    # and expanding attn mask for the new token, as explained above\n    attention_mask = inputs[\"attention_mask\"]\n    attention_mask = torch.cat([attention_mask, attention_mask.new_ones((attention_mask.shape[0], 1))], dim=-1)\n    inputs = {\"input_ids\": next_token_ids, \"attention_mask\": attention_mask}\n\nprint(tokenizer.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0])\n\"[INST] Hello, what's your name. [/INST]  Hello! My name is LLaMA,\"\n```\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 7977]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 7977, "selected_characters": 7977, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-TORCH-REPRO/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "4f6b1deb38f7f174a2e340674680aaa0484e02a1d18cccd1bb94618df512cc2a", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== docs/source/notes/randomness.rst =====\n.. _reproducibility:\n\nReproducibility\n===============\n\nCompletely reproducible results are not guaranteed across PyTorch releases,\nindividual commits, or different platforms. Furthermore, results may not be\nreproducible between CPU and GPU executions, even when using identical seeds.\n\nHowever, there are some steps you can take to limit the number of sources of\nnondeterministic behavior for a specific platform, device, and PyTorch release.\nFirst, you can control sources of randomness that can cause multiple executions\nof your application to behave differently. Second, you can configure PyTorch\nto avoid using nondeterministic algorithms for some operations, so that multiple\ncalls to those operations, given the same inputs, will produce the same result.\n\n.. warning::\n\n    Deterministic operations are often slower than nondeterministic operations, so\n    single-run performance may decrease for your model. However, determinism may\n    save time in development by facilitating experimentation, debugging, and\n    regression testing.\n\nControlling sources of randomness\n.................................\n\nPyTorch random number generator\n-------------------------------\nYou can use :meth:`torch.manual_seed()` to seed the RNG for all devices (both\nCPU and CUDA)::\n\n    import torch\n    torch.manual_seed(0)\n\nSome PyTorch operations may use random numbers internally.\n:meth:`torch.svd_lowrank()` does this, for instance. Consequently, calling it\nmultiple times back-to-back with the same input arguments may give different\nresults. However, as long as :meth:`torch.manual_seed()` is set to a constant\nat the beginning of an application and all other sources of nondeterminism have\nbeen eliminated, the same series of random numbers will be generated each time\nthe application is run in the same environment.\n\nIt is also possible to obtain identical results from an operation that uses\nrandom numbers by setting :meth:`torch.manual_seed()` to the same value between\nsubsequent calls.\n\nPython\n------\n\nFor custom operators, you might need to set python seed as well::\n\n    import random\n    random.seed(0)\n\nRandom number generators in other libraries\n-------------------------------------------\nIf you or any of the libraries you are using rely on NumPy, you can seed the global\nNumPy RNG with::\n\n    import numpy as np\n    np.random.seed(0)\n\nHowever, some applications and libraries may use NumPy Random Generator objects,\nnot the global RNG\n(`<https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/random/generator.html>`_), and those will\nneed to be seeded consistently as well.\n\nIf you are using any other libraries that use random number generators, refer to\nthe documentation for those libraries to see how to set consistent seeds for them.\n\nCUDA convolution benchmarking\n-----------------------------\nThe cuDNN library, used by CUDA convolution operations, can be a source of nondeterminism\nacross multiple executions of an application. When a cuDNN convolution is called with a\nnew set of size parameters, an optional feature can run multiple convolution algorithms,\nbenchmarking them to find the fastest one. Then, the fastest algorithm will be used\nconsistently during the rest of the process for the corresponding set of size parameters.\nDue to benchmarking noise and different hardware, the benchmark may select different\nalgorithms on subsequent runs, even on the same machine.\n\nDisabling the benchmarking feature with :code:`torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = False`\ncauses cuDNN to deterministically select an algorithm, possibly at the cost of reduced\nperformance.\n\nHowever, if you do not need reproducibility across multiple executions of your application,\nthen performance might improve if the benchmarking feature is enabled with\n:code:`torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = True`.\n\nNote that this setting is different from the :code:`torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic`\nsetting discussed below.\n\nAvoiding nondeterministic algorithms\n....................................\n:meth:`torch.use_deterministic_algorithms` lets you configure PyTorch to use\ndeterministic algorithms instead of nondeterministic ones where available, and\nto throw an error if an operation is known to be nondeterministic (and without\na deterministic alternative).\n\nPlease check the documentation for :meth:`torch.use_deterministic_algorithms()`\nfor a full list of affected operations. If an operation does not act correctly\naccording to the documentation, or if you need a deterministic implementation\nof an operation that does not have one, please submit an issue:\n`<https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues?q=label:%22module:%20determinism%22>`_\n\nFor example, running the nondeterministic CUDA implementation of :meth:`torch.Tensor.index_add_`\nwill throw an error::\n\n    >>> import torch\n    >>> torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)\n    >>> torch.randn(2, 2).cuda().index_add_(0, torch.tensor([0, 1]), torch.randn(2, 2))\n    Traceback (most recent call last):\n    File \"<stdin>\", line 1, in <module>\n    RuntimeError: index_add_cuda_ does not have a deterministic implementation, but you set\n    'torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)'. ...\n\nWhen :meth:`torch.bmm` is called with sparse-dense CUDA tensors it typically uses a\nnondeterministic algorithm, but when the deterministic flag is turned on, its alternate\ndeterministic implementation will be used::\n\n    >>> import torch\n    >>> torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)\n    >>> torch.bmm(torch.randn(2, 2, 2).to_sparse().cuda(), torch.randn(2, 2, 2).cuda())\n    tensor([[[ 1.1900, -2.3409],\n             [ 0.4796,  0.8003]],\n            [[ 0.1509,  1.8027],\n             [ 0.0333, -1.1444]]], device='cuda:0')\n\nCUDA convolution determinism\n----------------------------\nWhile disabling CUDA convolution benchmarking (discussed above) ensures that\nCUDA selects the same algorithm each time an application is run, that algorithm\nitself may be nondeterministic, unless either\n:code:`torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)` or\n:code:`torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic = True` is set. The latter setting\ncontrols only this behavior, unlike :meth:`torch.use_deterministic_algorithms`\nwhich will make other PyTorch operations behave deterministically, too.\n\nCUDA RNN and LSTM\n-----------------\nIn some versions of CUDA, RNNs and LSTM networks may have non-deterministic behavior.\nSee :meth:`torch.nn.RNN` and :meth:`torch.nn.LSTM` for details and workarounds.\n\nFilling uninitialized memory\n----------------------------\nOperations like :meth:`torch.empty` and :meth:`torch.Tensor.resize_` can return\ntensors with uninitialized memory that contain undefined values. Using such a\ntensor as an input to another operation is invalid if determinism is required,\nbecause the output will be nondeterministic. But there is nothing to actually\nprevent such invalid code from being run. So for safety,\n:attr:`torch.utils.deterministic.fill_uninitialized_memory` is set to ``True``\nby default, which will fill the uninitialized memory with a known value if\n:code:`torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)` is set. This will prevent the\npossibility of this kind of nondeterministic behavior.\n\nHowever, filling uninitialized memory is detrimental to performance. So if your\nprogram is valid and does not use uninitialized memory as the input to an\noperation, then this setting can be turned off for better performance.\n\nDataLoader\n..........\n\nDataLoader will reseed workers following :ref:`data-loading-randomness` algorithm.\nUse :meth:`worker_init_fn` and `generator` to preserve reproducibility::\n\n    def seed_worker(worker_id):\n        worker_seed = torch.initial_seed() % 2**32\n        numpy.random.seed(worker_seed)\n        random.seed(worker_seed)\n\n    g = torch.Generator()\n    g.manual_seed(0)\n\n    DataLoader(\n        train_dataset,\n        batch_size=batch_size,\n        num_workers=num_workers,\n        worker_init_fn=seed_worker,\n        generator=g,\n    )\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 7936]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 7936, "selected_characters": 7936, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-EAGLE/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "509e09a8a7d28806b4154100599a018a8535fd4cccbd56129efa10ea90436df2", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "=== PDF page 1 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nYuhui Li♠\nFangyun Wei‡\nChao Zhang♠\nHongyang Zhang♣†\n♠Peking University\n‡Microsoft Research\n♣University of Waterloo\n†Vector Institute\nhongyang.zhang@uwaterloo.ca\nhttps://github.com/SafeAILab/EAGLE\nFigure 1: Speedup ratio of Vicuna and LLaMA2-Chat inference latency on the MT-bench for greedy (temperature=0)\nsettings. Speedup ratio of Medusa and Lookahead are copied from their original technical reports. With speculative sampling,\nthere is a lack of suitable draft models to accelerate the 7B model. Employing a 7B model as the draft model for a 13B\nmodel results in slow speeds due to the high overhead of the 7B model, rendering it less efficient than vanilla auto-regressive\ndecoding. These scenarios are marked as N/A. In this paper, we only compare methods that do not need to finetune the\nbackbone models, ensuring the output text distribution remains constant.\nAbstract\nAuto-regressive decoding makes the inference of\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) time-consuming.\nWe propose a simple framework, EAGLE (Extrap-\nolation Algorithm for Greater Language-model\nEfficiency), for lossless acceleration. Unlike tra-\nditional speculative sampling methods, EAGLE\noperates the drafting process auto-regressively at\nthe more regular (second-top-layer) feature level\nand addresses the sampling uncertainty issues in\nthe next-feature prediction problems by integrat-\ning tokens from one time step ahead. The acceler-\nation provided by EAGLE is lossless: it involves\nno fine-tuning of the target LLM, and the gener-\nated text maintains the same distribution as that\nof vanilla auto-regressive decoding. As of the sub-\nmission of this paper, EAGLE is the fastest known\nframework within the speculative sampling family.\nOn MT-bench, EAGLE is 3x faster than vanilla de-\ncoding, 2x faster than Lookahead, and 1.6x faster\nthan Medusa. Using gpt-fast, EAGLE attains on\naverage 160 tokens/s with LLaMA2-Chat 13B on\na single RTX 3090 GPU, compared to 24 tokens/s\nof Huggingface’s implementations.\n1. Introduction\nAuto-regressive decoding has become the de facto standard\nfor large language models (LLMs). This process gener-\nates output tokens one at a time, which makes the genera-\ntion by LLMs both costly and slow. Speculative sampling\n(Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023a) based methods\noffer a solution to this challenge. They divide the generation\nprocess of LLMs into two stages: the draft stage, where\npotential tokens are conjectured at a low cost, and the veri-\nfication stage, where these tokens are validated in parallel\nthrough a single forward pass of the LLM. The paralleliza-\ntion in speculative sampling facilitates the generation of\n1\narXiv:2401.15077v1  [cs.LG]  26 Jan 2024\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nmultiple post-check tokens per LLM forward pass, signifi-\ncantly enhancing speed. More importantly, the verification\nstage in speculative sampling ensures that the text distribu-\ntion aligns precisely with the decoding results of the original\nLLM, maintaining the integrity of the generated content.\nThe key to applying speculative sampling is to identify a\ndraft model that is sufficiently similar to the original LLM\nyet has lower latency. Speculative sampling typically em-\nploys a lower-parameter version of a LLM from the same\nseries as the draft model. For instance, in the LLaMA2\n(Touvron et al., 2023) series which includes models with\n7B, 13B, and 70B parameters, using the 7B model as a\nproxy of the 70B model is valid, while finding a suitable\ndraft model for the smallest 7B variant is tricky. An alter-\nnative could be to use TinyLLaMA (Zhang et al., 2024),\nbut this is not feasible for instruct-tuned models due to the\ninconsistency in instruction templates between LLaMA2-\nChat and TinyLLaMA-Chat. For 13B and 70B models, a 7B\nmodel within the same series could serve as the draft model.\nHowever, due to the high overhead of the 7B model, the\nacceleration effect of speculative sampling is not optimal.\nTraining a new, appropriately sized draft model specifically\nfor speculative sampling is not an ideal solution either due\nto the high cost: TinyLLaMA is trained on 3,000B tokens,\nwhereas EAGLE is trained on 2-4B tokens.\nThe key to enhancing acceleration in speculative sampling\nlies in reducing the time overhead and improving the ac-\nceptance rate of the draft by the original LLM (Chen et al.,\n2023b; Xia et al., 2023; Santilli et al., 2023). Numerous ap-\nproaches focus on reducing the overhead of the draft phase.\nLookahead (Fu et al., 2023) employs n-gram and Jacobi iter-\nation, while Medusa (Cai et al., 2023) utilizes a set of MLPs\nthat predict tokens based on the second-top-layer feature of\nthe original LLM. These strategies significantly decrease\nthe latency in generating drafts, leading to improved accel-\neration. However, the ultimate potential of these methods is\ncapped due to the lower accuracy of the drafts they produce.\nBuilding upon the observation that auto-regression at the\nfeature level is more manageable than at the token level,\nwe introduce a simple framework, EAGLE (Extrapolation\nAlgorithm for Greater Language-model Efficiency), which\ndiverges from direct token prediction and instead performs\nauto-regressive operations at the feature level. By incorpo-\nrating a token sequence advanced by one time step, EAGLE\ncircumvents the uncertainty associated with feature-level\nauto-regression. With the draft model comprising merely a\ntransformer decoder layer, EAGLE achieves a draft accuracy\nof approximately 0.8, substantially surpassing Medusa’s 0.6.\nWe evaluated EAGLE on the MT-bench (Zheng et al., 2023),\na highly realistic benchmark simulating actual applications\nand real-world scenarios, including multi-turn instructions\nakin to dialogues with ChatGPT. We have chosen to uti-\nFigure 2: Speedup ratio on the MT-bench for non-greedy\n(temperature=1) settings. Lookahead is confined to greedy\ndecoding, and the non-greedy generation of Medusa does\nnot guarantee lossless performance. Therefore, EAGLE is\nnot compared with these methods.\nlize this benchmark as it has been employed by the cur-\nrent state-of-the-art, including Lookahead and Medusa, to\ndemonstrate their speedup ratios. This choice facilitates\na fair and direct comparison between our approach and\nthese benchmarks. Under a greedy decoding setting, for\nVicuna-13B and LLaMA2-Chat 13B, 70B, EAGLE offers a\n3x acceleration that is theoretically guaranteed to maintain\ntext distribution of the original LLM and is ready to use\nright out–of-the-box. Compared to the recently proposed\nspeculative sampling-based frameworks, Lookahead and\nMedusa, EAGLE achieves 2x and 1.6x speedups, respec-\ntively. While enhancing speed, EAGLE also increases the\nthroughput of LLM systems by 2x. EAGLE operates in\nparallel with other acceleration or throughput-improving\nmethods, such as quantization, compilation, etc. Combining\nEAGLE with these techniques could further reduce the oper-\national costs of LLM systems. For example, with gpt-fast1,\nEAGLE accelerates LLaMA2-Chat 7B decoding from 24.5\ntokens/s to 160.4 tokens/s on a single RTX 3090 GPU.\nEAGLE boasts low training costs. For the LLaMA2-Chat\n70B model, EAGLE trains a decoder layer with fewer than\n1B parameters using no more than 70k dialogues from the\nShareGPT dataset. The training is completed in 1-2 days\non 4x A100 (40G) GPUs. In practical applications, EAGLE\nrequires only a single training session to provide accelera-\ntion for each query. As the number of queries increases, the\namortized training cost of EAGLE becomes negligible.\nCompared with existing speculative sampling-based tech-\nniques, the advantages of EAGLE include:\n• Simplicity: EAGLE is a new framework to accelerate\nauto-regressive decoding and is applicable to any auto-\nregressive LLMs. The method adds only a lightweight\nplug-in (a single transformer decoder layer) to the\nLLM, which can be easily deployed in a production\n1https://github.com/pytorch-labs/gpt-fast\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nenvironment. In this paper, we have applied EAGLE to\nLLaMA2-Chat (7B, 13B, 70B), Vicuna (7B, 13B, 33B)\nand Mixtral 8x7B Instruct-v0.1 in a zero-shot way on\nthe MT-bench and GSM8K datasets.\n• Reliability: EAGLE does not involve any fine-tuning\nof the original LLM, and the preservation of the output\ndistribution by EAGLE is theoretically guaranteed for\nboth the greedy and non-greedy settings. This ensures\nno risk of degradation in edge cases, where acceleration\nmight lead to erroneous or even harmful LLM outputs.\nThis is in sharp contrast to Lookahead and Medusa\nwhich focuses on greedy settings only.\n• Speed: EAGLE stands out as the fastest framework\nwithin the family of speculative sampling as of the\nsubmission of this paper. Notably, on the Vicuna and\nLLaMA2-Chat for greedy inference (see Figure 1), EA-\nGLE surpasses vanilla decoding speeds by a factor of\n3, outpaces Lookahead decoding by 2 times, and is\n1.6 times faster than Medusa decoding. Using gpt-fast,\nEAGLE attains on average 160 tokens/s with LLaMA2-\nChat 13B on a single RTX 3090 GPU, compared to\n24 tokens/s of Huggingface’s implementations. In the\nMoE (Mixture of Experts) model, specifically the Mix-\ntral 8x7B Instruct-v0.1 (Jiang et al., 2024), the EAGLE\nalgorithm achieves a speed increase of 1.5 times.\nIn addition to introducing the simple yet efficient frame-\nwork of EAGLE, we also provide an analysis of the factors\ncontributing to its effectiveness, which might be of indepen-\ndent interest to other speculative sampling methods (Section\n5.3.2). EAGLE is built upon the following two observations:\n• Firstly, utilizing top-layer features proves to be more\neffective than employing bottom-layer token embed-\ndings when using the same lightweight network.\n• Secondly, the uncertainty inherent in the sampling pro-\ncess significantly constrains the performance of draft\nmodels that only input top-layer features. Hence, in-\ncorporating the sampling results (token) into the draft\nmodel is crucial.\n2. Preliminaries\nNotations. In this paper, we refer to the Large Language\nModel intended for acceleration as the “target LLM” and the\nmodel used for rapid draft generation as the “draft model”.\nUnless specified otherwise, “feature” refers to the second-\nto-top-layer feature of LLM, that is, the hidden state before\nthe LM Head. We denote tokens by lowercase letters t, their\nembeddings by e, and features by f, and the distribution\nby p. We use uppercase letters to represent sequences. For\ninstance, Ti:j denotes the sequence (ti, ti+1, . . . , tj). Con-\nsidering a LLM, given an input token sequence T1:j, the\nLLM acquires corresponding embeddings E1:j through the\nembedding layer. These are processed through multiple de-\ncoder layers to yield features F1:j. The LM Head then maps\nfj onto a distribution pj+1 = LM Head(fj), from which\nthe next token tj+1 is sampled. As an example, vanilla\nauto-regression at the token level can be expressed by the\nprocess (T1:j →E1:j →fj →pj+1 →tj+1) for any\ninteger j ≥1.\nSpeculative sampling. Speculative sampling alternates be-\ntween the draft and verification phases. Consider the current\nsequence T1:j. During the draft phase, speculative sam-\npling employs a model, smaller than the target LLM, to\nauto-regressively generate γ tokens ˆTj+1:j+γ,2 and records\nthe corresponding distributions ˆPj+1:j+γ. In the verifica-\ntion phase, a single forward pass of the target LLM yields\nthe probabilities for the corresponding positions. During\nthe verification phase, a single forward pass of the target\nLLM provides the probabilities for the corresponding po-\nsitions. Tokens are then sequentially evaluated for accep-\ntance, with token ˆtj+i having an acceptance probability\nmin(1, pj+i/ˆpj+i). Upon the rejection of a token ˆtj+i, all\nsubsequent tokens are discarded, and this token is resam-\npled based on a distribution norm(max(0, pj+i −ˆpj+i)).\nAppendix A.1 of speculative sampling (Leviathan et al.,\n2023) proves that the aforementioned sampling method is\nequivalent to directly sampling from the distribution of the\ntarget LLM. EAGLE also employs this sampling approach,\nensuring that the distribution of the generated text re-\nmains unchanged for both the greedy and non-greedy\nsettings.\n3. Observations\nWe begin with two observations that directly motivate the\ndesign of EAGLE.\n3.1. Auto-regressing features is easier than tokens\nToken sequences represent simple transformations of nat-\nural language and are inherently complex. In contrast, the\nsecond-top-layer features of LLMs, after undergoing a lin-\near transformation (LM Head), can predict the next token,\nmaking the feature sequences more structured and regu-\nlar. Consequently, performing auto-regression at the feature\nlevel is expected to be more manageable than at the token\nlevel. We hypothesize this is because high-level feature’s\nevolution path over time is simpler than that of tokens, thus\nthe former can be characterized by a smaller model. To\nvalidate this hypothesis, we designed experiments on MT-\n2We will frequently use the hat notations to denote the corre-\nsponding terms for the draft models.\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nFigure 3: Accuracy and speedup ratio of draft models based\non tokens and features, tested on MT-bench with Vicuna 7B\nas the target LLM.\nbench with Vicuna 7B (Chiang et al., 2023) by setting the\ndraft models as a one-layer transformer decoder, for both\nthe feature and token auto-regression. As illustrated in Fig-\nure 3, auto-regressive feature processing followed by token\ngeneration ((f1, f2) →f3 →t4) using the frozen LM Head\nyields a much higher accuracy compared to auto-regressive\ntoken processing ((t1, t2, t3) →t4), with an improvement\nas large as 30%. This results in a much higher speedup ratio\ntoo (1.5x→1.9x).\n3.2. Uncertainty matters in next-feature predictions\nWhen generating text, the target LLM does not directly\npredict tokens; instead, it forecasts the probability distri-\nbution of tokens and then samples based on this distribu-\ntion, thereby introducing randomness into the generation\nprocess. Unlike tokens, features are high-dimensional and\ncontinuous, precluding the use of the same sampling-based\napproach. Consider the example illustrated in Figure 4. If\n“am” is sampled, the feature sequence becomes (fI, fam).\nConversely, if “always” is sampled, the feature sequence\nbecomes (fI, falways). This introduces ambiguity into the\nauto-regressive model of feature sequences: it is uncertain\nwhether the next feature following fI should be fam or\nfalways. Medusa also confronts this issue. Its objective is\nto predict the probability of tokens spaced by an interval,\nsuch as using fI to predict pam or palways in this example,\ncorresponding to the probability of the next token following\n“I am” or “I always”, respectively. This inherent uncertainty\nrenders it impossible for Medusa’s draft model to perfectly\nemulate the target LLM. To address this issue, EAGLE in-\nputs the token sequence from one time step ahead, which\nincludes the sampling outcomes, into the draft model. In\nthe example illustrated in Figure 4, this involves predicting\nfalways based on fI and talways, and predicting fam based\non fI and tam. As illustrated in Figure 5, by addressing the\nuncertainty, the speedup ratio further increases from 1.9x to\n2.8x.\n4. EAGLE\nEAGLE is built upon the above two observations. It encom-\npasses both a drafting phase and a verification phase, to be\nI\n𝑒I\n𝑓I\n𝑝(am)=0.6 \n𝑝(always)=0.4 \nsampling\nam\n𝑒am\n𝑓am\n𝑝(excited)=0.3 \n𝑝(ready)=0.7 \nsampling\nalways\n𝑒always\n𝑓always\n𝑝(begin)=0.8 \n𝑝(look)=0.2 \n𝑝always\n𝑝I\n𝑝am\nFigure 4: Uncertainty in feature sequences. The next fea-\nture following fI is contingent on the sampling outcome\nand cannot be determined solely based on fI, where both\n“always” and “am” are possible to follow the token “I” and\nlead to two branches.\nFigure 5: Accuracy and speedup ratio of draft models based\non feature and feature&shifted-token. The feature&shifted-\ntoken approach effectively resolves the uncertainty. The\nmodels were tested on MT-bench, employing Vicuna 7B as\nthe target LLM.\nconsistent with other speculative sampling based methods.\n4.1. Drafting phase\nThe primary distinction between EAGLE and other methods\nlies predominantly in the drafting phase. Figure 6 illustrates\na schematic of the drafting phase for different methods.\nSpeculative sampling (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al.,\n2023a) and Lookahead (Fu et al., 2023) predict tokens based\non tokens. Medusa (Cai et al., 2023) independently predicts\nt4 and t5 using the feature f2 from the target LLM. EAGLE\npredicts f3 using the feature sequence (f1, f2) and the token\nsequence (t2, t3), advanced by one time step. From p4 =\nLM Head(f3), t4 is sampled. Subsequently, f3 and t4 are\nconcatenated into the input sequence to predict the next\nfeature f4 and sample the subsequent token t5.\nAs illustrated in Figure 7, EAGLE’s draft model comprises\nthree modules: the Embedding layer, LM Head, and Auto-\nregression Head. The Embedding layer and LM Head em-\nploy the parameters of the original LLM and do not neces-\nsitate additional training. The draft model takes as input a\nfeature sequence of shape (bs, seq len, hidden dim) and an\nadvanced token sequence of shape (bs, seq len). It then con-\nverts the token sequence into a token embedding sequence\nof shape (bs, seq len, hidden dim), and concatenates it to\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\n𝑡1\n𝑡2\n𝑡3\nSmaller LLM\n𝑡4\n𝑡1\n𝑡2\n𝑡3\nSmaller LLM\n𝑡5\n𝑡4\nSpeculative Sampling\n𝑡3\n2-Gram, Jacobi\n𝑡4\n2-Gram, Jacobi\n𝑡5\n𝑡4\nLookahead\nMedusa\nEmbedding layer &\nAuto-regression Head\n𝑓3\n𝑓4\nEAGLE\n𝑡4\n𝑡5\n𝑓1\n𝑡2\n𝑓2\n𝑡3\n𝑓2\n𝑡3\n𝑓3\n𝑡4\n𝑓1\n𝑡2\nMedusa Head1\n𝑡4\n𝑓2\n𝑡5\nMedusa Head2\nEmbedding layer &\nAuto-regression Head\nFigure 6: A comparison of the methods for drafting the\nfourth and fifth tokens, t4 and t5, where (t1, t2) is the\nprompt. t (represented by blue blocks) denotes tokens, and\nf (orange blocks) signifies the features, with subscripts in-\ndicating their positions in the sequence. The red border\nindicates the predictions of the draft model. For simplicity,\nthe n in the n-gram for Lookahead, as shown in the figure,\nhas been set to 2.\nform a fused sequence of shape (bs, seq len, 2×hidden dim).\nThe Auto-regression Head consisting of an FC layer and a\ndecoder layer. The FC layer reduces the dimensionality of\nthe fused sequence to (bs, seq len, hidden dim) and then we\nutilize the decoder layer to predict the next feature. The LM\nHead calculates the distribution based on the feature, from\nwhich the next token is sampled. Finally, the predicted fea-\nture and the sampled token are concatenated into the input,\nfacilitating the continuation of the auto-regressive process.\nEAGLE generates a tree-structured draft. To enhance effi-\nciency, we implement tree attention, enabling the creation\nof a draft tree with a depth of m through m forward passes,\nthereby encompassing more than m tokens. In the example\nillustrated in Figure 7, EAGLE drafts a tree of 10 tokens\nthrough 3 forward passes.\n4.2. Training of the draft models\nPredicting the next feature constitutes a regression task, for\nwhich we employ Smooth L1 loss (see Figure 6 EAGLE):\nE2:i+1 = Token Embedding(T2:i+1),\nˆfi+1 = Auto-regression Head(E2:i+1, F1:i),\nLreg = Smooth L1(fi+1, ˆfi+1).\nPredicting features is an intermediary objective of the draft\nmodel, with the ultimate goal being the prediction of tokens\nto generate a sequence of tokens. Consequently, we also\nemploy classification loss to directly optimize towards this\nfinal objective:\nEmbedding\nTransformer \nLayers\nHow\ncan\n𝑒how\n𝑒can\n𝑓how\n𝑓can\nLM Head\ncan\nI\nEmbedding\nOne Auto-regression Head\nI\nhelp\n𝑒I\n𝑒help\n𝑓I\n𝑓help\nLM Head\nmake/\nhelp\n𝑓can\nSampling multiple times\nSampling\n𝑓I\nwith/\nyou\nmake\n𝑒make\n𝑓make\n𝑓I\na/\nour\nwith\n𝑒with\n𝑓with\n𝑓help\nthe/\nyour\nyou\n𝑒you\n𝑓you\n𝑓help\nto/\nfeel\ntarget LLM\nDraft model\nHow can\nI\nhelp\nmake\nwith\nyou\nthe\nyour\nto\nfeel\na\nour\nQuery\nSampling using Original LLM\nDrafting using \nFeatExtrapolator\nForward 1\nForward 1\nForward 2\nForward 3\nForward 1\nForward 1\nForward 2\nForward 3\ncan\n𝑒can\n𝑓how\nFigure 7: Pipeline of EAGLE. The upper section illustrates\nthe computational process, while the lower section displays\nthe corresponding generation results for each step. In the\nupper section, green blocks represent token embeddings,\norange blocks signify the features from the second-top-layer\nof the LLM, red boxes indicate features predicted by the\nAuto-regression Head, and blue modules with snowflake\nicons represent the use of original LLM parameters, which\nare not subject to training.\npi+2 = Softmax(LM Head(fi+1)),\nˆpi+2 = Softmax(LM Head( ˆfi+1)),\nLcls = Cross Entropy(pi+2, ˆpi+2).\nBy integrating regression loss and classification loss, we\ntrain the Auto-regression Head using the following com-\nbined loss function:\nL = Lreg + wclsLcls.\nTypically, the classification loss is an order of magnitude\nlarger than the regression loss in numerical terms. Conse-\nquently, we set wcls to 0.1.\nThe optimal approach for training the Auto-regression Head\ninvolves generating text auto-regressively using the target\nLLM. However, such data generation is costly. Fortunately,\nEAGLE exhibits low sensitivity to training data (ablation\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nstudy in Section 5.3.3). Instead of employing text generated\nby the target LLM, we utilize a fixed dataset, substantially\nreducing the overhead. During the drafting phase, EAGLE\nauto-regressively processes features. Inaccuracies in fea-\ntures can lead to error accumulation. To mitigate this is-\nsue, we employ data augmentation by adding random noise\nsampled from a uniform distribution U(−0.1, 0.1) to the\nsecond-top-layer feature vectors of the target LLM during\ntraining (Jain et al., 2023).\n4.3. Verification phase\nEmploying tree attention, the target LLM computes the\nprobability of each token in the tree-structured draft through\na single forward pass. At every level of the draft tree, we\nrecursively apply speculative sampling algorithms to sample\nor adjust the distribution, consistent with SpecInfer (Miao\net al., 2023) and SpecTr (Sun et al., 2023), ensuring that\nthe distribution of the output text aligns with that of the\noriginal LLM. Simultaneously, we record the accepted to-\nkens and their corresponding features, which serve as the\nstarting point for auto-regressive feature extrapolation in the\nsubsequent draft phase.\n5. Experiments\nModels and tasks. We conducted experiments on Vicuna\nmodels (with sizes of 7B, 13B, 33B), LLaMA2-chat models\n(7B, 13B, 70B) and Mixtral 8x7B Instruct-v0.1, encom-\npassing the common sizes of current mainstream Large\nLanguage Models (LLMs). Our evaluations span dialogue\nand mathematical reasoning tasks. For dialogue tasks, we\nutilized the MT-bench, which comprises highly realistic sit-\nuational questions designed to test a model’s capabilities in\nmulti-turn conversations and adherence to instructions. For\nmathematical reasoning tasks, we employed the GSM8K\n(Cobbe et al., 2021) dataset, consisting of high-quality grade\nschool math problems created by human problem writers.\nSpeculative sampling (Leviathan et al., 2023) conducted\nexperiments with a batch size of 1, a setting subsequently\nadopted by other works such as DistillSpec (Zhou et al.,\n2023) and BiLD (Kim et al., 2023). Similarly, the majority\nof our experiments also adopted this setting. Experiments\nwith a batch size greater than 1 are presented in Section 5.4.\nMetrics. Similar to other methods based on speculative\nsampling, EAGLE primarily focuses on latency rather than\nthroughput. Following the methodology of DistillSpec, we\nassess acceleration effects using the following metrics:\n• Walltime speedup ratio: The actual test speedup ratio\nrelative to vanilla auto-regressive decoding.\n• Average acceptance length τ: The average number of\ntokens accepted per forward pass of the original LLM.\nFigure 8: Speedup ratios of EAGLE across various tasks.\n• Acceptance rate α: The ratio of the number of tokens\naccepted to the number of tokens generated during the\ndraft phase. The acceptance rate metric measures the\naccuracy of drafts. For tree draft, multiple tokens may\nbe sampled at the same location, yet only one token\nis accepted, rendering the acceptance rate metric un-\nsuitable for assessment in this context. Therefore, for\nthe experiments reported in this paper concerning ac-\nceptance rate, we do not use tree draft but rather chain\ndraft, similar to speculative sampling and DistillSpec.\nThe draft model of EAGLE takes feature sequence and\na token sequence as input. The auto-regressive pro-\ncessing of features in the draft phase can lead to the\npropagation of errors. We define the Acceptance rate\nwhen the input contains n features predicted by the\ndraft model, which may include errors, as n-α.\nAcceleration of EAGLE theoretically guarantees the preser-\nvation of the original LLMs’ output distribution. Conse-\nquently, evaluating the quality of EAGLE’s generated results\nis both unnecessary and meaningless.\nTraining. We fixed the target LLMs. EAGLE was trained on\nthe ShareGPT platform, utilizing 68,000 dialogue iterations\nwith a learning rate set at 3e-5. We employed the AdamW\noptimizer with beta values (β1, β2) set to (0.9, 0.95) and\nimplemented gradient clipping of 0.5. The trainable parame-\nters of EAGLE corresponding to the 7B, 13B, 33B, and 70B\nmodels are 0.24B, 0.37B, 0.56B, and 0.99B, respectively.\nThe trainable parameters of EAGLE for MoE model Mixtral\n8x7B is 0.28B. EAGLE is characterized by its low training\ncost; the Auto-regression Head is trainable within 1-2 days\non an A100 40G server for the 70B models.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\n5.1. Effectiveness\nFigure 1 illustrates the speedup ratios of EAGLE, Medusa,\nand Lookahead on the MT-bench, with temperature=0. On\nthe Vicuna 13B and LLaMA2-Chat 13B, 70B models, EA-\nGLE achieves a speed that is 3x faster than vanilla auto-\nregressive decoding, 2x faster than Lookahead, and 1.6x\nfaster than Medusa. Figure 2 and Table 2 showcase the\nacceleration ratios of EAGLE under various settings. Fig-\nure 8 presents the speedup ratios of EAGLE across vari-\nous tasks. The coding task, which involves a substantial\nnumber of fixed templates, exhibits the most significant\nspeedup effect. EAGLE also demonstrates notable effec-\ntiveness in other tasks. Tables 1 display the Average accep-\ntance length and acceptance rate of EAGLE. The original\nLLM generates nearly four tokens per forward pass, which\nis a significant efficiency improvement compared to the\nvanilla auto-regressive decoding that generates one token\nper pass. The acceptance rate with completely accurate\nfeature sequences, 0-α, is notably higher than that with\na single erroneous feature, 1-α, indicating that errors in\nfeatures can impact performance of EAGLE. However, the\nminimal differences between 1-α, 2-α, 3-α, and 4-α demon-\nstrate EAGLE’s robustness to feature errors and its ability\nto effectively manage the accumulation of such errors.\nTable 1: Average acceptance length τ and acceptance rate α\non MT-bench. Ablation study\n5.3.1. TREE ATTENTION\nEAGLE, similar to SpecInfer and Medusa, employs tree\nattention, where both the generation and validation of\ndrafts are tree-structured. In contrast, methods like specula-\ntive sampling do not use tree attention, resulting in chain-\nstructured draft generation and validation. Table 5 and Fig-\nure 9 present comparative results indicating the impact of\nusing tree attention. The implementation of tree draft and\nverification in EAGLE results in an approximate increase of\n0.6-0.8 in the average acceptance length and about 0.3-0.5\nin the speedup ratio. Compared to chain draft and verifica-\ntion, tree draft and verification do not increase the number\nof forward passes in the model (both the target LLM and\nthe draft model), but they do increase the number of tokens\nprocessed per forward pass. Consequently, the improvement\nin the speedup ratio is less pronounced than the increase\nin average acceptance length. Notably, even without em-\nploying tree draft and verification, EAGLE demonstrates a\nsignificant acceleration effect, approximately in the range\nof 2.2x-2.7x.\n5.3.2. INPUTS OF DRAFT MODELS\nCompared to other speculative sampling-based methods,\nthe key innovation of EAGLE lies in its utilization of fea-\ntures computed by the target LLM and the incorporation\nof sampling outcomes into the input of the draft model\nto address randomness. We conducted an ablation study\non Vicuna 7B, assessing draft models with varying inputs.\nWe tested four types of inputs: feature&shifted-token (EA-\nGLE), feature&unshifted-token, token, and feature. Both\nfeature&shifted-token (EAGLE) and feature&unshifted-\ntoken integrate semantic information at different levels. The\ndistinction lies in the fact that feature&shifted-token (EA-\nGLE) inputs tokens advanced by one time step, equipping\nit to address randomness effectively. Apart from the use of\na FC layer to reduce dimensionality for the feature&token\ninput, the structure of the draft model remains entirely con-\nsistent. Figure 10 presents the experimental outcomes on\nthe MT-bench with Vicuna 7B as the target LLM. Three\nobservations can be drawn.\n• First, when the number of parameters of the draft\nmodel is limited, utilizing features yields slightly better\nresults than tokens.\n• Second, integrating features and tokens slightly en-\nhances performance. This is primarily because discrete,\nerror-free tokens mitigate the accumulation of errors in\nfeatures (as evidenced by the near-equivalence of the\nacceptance rate of the feature&unshifted-token draft\nmodel to that of the feature-only model, 0-α, but with\na markedly higher 1-α).\n• Third, addressing the randomness inherent in the sam-\npling process results in the most significant improve-\nment. The feature&shifted-token scheme, compared\nto feature&unshifted-token, adds no complexity yet\nmarkedly enhances the draft model’s capability by sim-\nply advancing the token by one time step, allowing the\ndraft model to account for the randomness in sampling.\n5.3.3. TRAINING DATA\nEAGLE utilizes a fixed dataset for training. Employing the\ntarget LLM for auto-regressive decoding on dataset queries\nto generate training data may yield enhanced performance\nbut would substantially increase training overhead. We con-\nducted ablation experiments on the MT-bench dataset. As\nshown in Table 6, training the draft model with data gener-\nated by the target LLM leads to only a slight improvement\nin performance. This suggests that EAGLE is not sensitive\nto the training data, allowing for the adoption of a fixed\ndataset approach. Consequently, this choice significantly\nreduces the training costs of EAGLE.\n5.4. Batch size and throughput\nInference in LLMs is memory-bound (Patterson, 2004;\nShazeer, 2019), leaving GPU computational resources un-\nderutilized. The principle behind the speculative sampling-\nbased approach in enhancing generation speed lies in more\neffectively utilizing GPU computational resources. As the\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n1.5\n2.0\n2.5\nT = 0\nSpeedup\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n2\n3\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.4\n0.6\n0.8\n0-\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\n1-\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n1.0\n1.5\n2.0\nT = 1\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n1.5\n2.0\n2.5\n3.0\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\n2.5\n5.0\nEpoch\n0.2\n0.4\n0.6\nfeature&shifted-token\nfeature&unshifted-token\ntoken\nfeature\nFigure 10: Performance of draft models with varying inputs. The target LLM is Vicuna 7B, and the test dataset is MT-bench.\nSpeed refers to the walltime speedup ratio, τ denotes the average acceptance length, 0-α represents the acceptance rate with\nentirely precise inputs, 1-α indicates the acceptance rate when the input includes one imprecise feature, and T refers to the\ntemperature.\nTable 6: The speedup ratios and average acceptance length τ\nusing different training datasets evaluated on the MT-bench,\nwith the target LLM being LLaMA2-Chat 7B and the tem-\nperature set to 0. “Fixed dataset” refers to both questions\nand answers originating from the ShareGPT dataset. “Data\ngenerated by target LLM” denotes that while questions are\nsourced from the ShareGPT dataset, the answers are gener-\nated by the target LLM.\nTraining data\nSpeedup\nτ\nFixed dataset\n2.78x\n3.62\nData generated by target LLM\n2.88x\n3.75\nbatch size increases, the available computational capacity\nof the GPU decreases, leading to a reduction in the ac-\nceleration effect. In this section, we present experimental\nresults for scenarios where the batch size exceeds 1. As\ndemonstrated in Table 7, the speedup ratio diminishes with\nincreasing batch size. When using Vicuna 7B as the target\nLLM, the speedup ratio at bs=4 is higher than at bs=3. This\nis attributed to the fact that, during the verification phase\nof EAGLE, the target LLM processes multiple tokens in a\nsingle forward pass, and the processing at bs=4 is faster than\nat bs=3. In contrast, with vanilla auto-regressive decoding\nwhere the target LLM processes one token per forward pass,\nthe speeds at bs=3 and bs=4 are nearly identical.\nAlthough speculative sampling-based methods predomi-\nnantly focus on latency, we also investigated EAGLE’s\nthroughput for batch size > 1, another key metric for LLM\nsystems. Compared to vanilla auto-regressive decoding,\nEAGLE requires roughly the same CUDA memory. For\nVicuna 7B as the target LLM, operating under a memory\nconstraint of a single RTX 3090 with 24G of CUDA mem-\nory, the maximum batch size (bs) for vanilla auto-regressive\ndecoding and EAGLE are 8 and 7, respectively. In the\ncase of LLaMA2-Chat 70B, constrained by 4 A100 (40G)\nGPUs totaling 160G of CUDA memory, the maximum bs\nfor vanilla auto-regressive decoding and EAGLE are 5 and\n4, respectively. All evaluations were conducted at FP16\nprecision. We calculated the throughput for different bs and\nselected the maximum value. Both vanilla auto-regressive\ndecoding and EAGLE achieve maximum throughput at their\nrespective maximum bs. Tree attention consumes more\ncomputational resources. At a batch of bs=7, the computa-\ntional resources are less abundant, making the non-use of\ntree attention more advantageous. As illustrated in Table 7,\nEAGLE achieves a 2x increase in throughput.\n6. Related Work\nThere has been considerable research into accelerating lan-\nguage models, involving techniques such as distillation (Hin-\nton et al., 2015), quantization (Hubara et al., 2018). These\nmethods aim to reduce the latency per forward pass.\n9\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\nEAGLE: Speculative Sampling Requires Rethinking Feature Uncertainty\nTable 7: Speedup ratios at different batch sizes and through-\nput of EAGLE. The evaluation dataset is MT-bench, with\nthe temperature parameter set to 0.\nBatch size\n1\n2\n3\n4\nThroughput\nVicuna 7B\n2.90x\n2.87x\n2.65x\n2.76x\n1.97x\nLLaMA2-Chat 70B\n3.01x\n2.81x\n2.50x\n2.40x\n1.99x\nSimilar to our approach are frameworks based on specula-\ntive sampling. Early works (Stern et al., 2018; Sun et al.,\n2021) accelerated greedy decoding, while speculative sam-\npling (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023a) extended it\nto non-greedy sampling, provably maintaining the original\noutput distribution. Ensuring unchanged output distribution\nmakes acceleration more challenging; many studies have\nexplored lossy acceleration as a trade-off. For instance,\nDistillSpec (Zhou et al., 2023) modifies acceptance proba-\nbilities using a lenience function, BiLD (Kim et al., 2023)\naccepts drafts if the distance metric from the original LLM\ndistribution is below a certain threshold, and Medusa (Cai\net al., 2023) uses a minimum of a hard threshold and an\nentropy-dependent threshold for truncation. In contrast, EA-\nGLE does not employ any relaxations and maintains the\noutput distribution of the LLM unchanged.\nThe primary differences among speculative sampling-based\nmethods manifest predominantly in the draft phase. Specu-\nlative sampling (Leviathan et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023a)\nutilizes a lower-parameter version of the original LLM as\nthe draft model. Self-Speculative Decoding (Zhang et al.,\n2023) skips some layers of the original LLM during draft\ngeneration. SpecInfer (Miao et al., 2023) employs a set of\nsmall models to generate drafts in parallel. Cascade Specu-\nlative Drafting (Chen et al., 2023b) and Staged Speculative\nDecoding (Spector & Re, 2023) cascade different overhead\ndraft models. Online Speculative Decoding (Liu et al., 2023)\ntrains the draft model on a distribution of queries. Methods\n(Hooper et al., 2023; Fu et al., 2023; Yang et al., 2023b) such\nas Medusa (Cai et al., 2023) do not employ a separate target\nLLM; instead, they generate drafts by utilizing features or\nweights from the target LLM. REST (He et al., 2023) gener-\nates drafts based on retrieval methods. LLMA (Yang et al.,\n2023a), used for tasks like grammatical correction where\ninput and output overlap, retrieves drafts directly from the\ninput.\n7. Conclusion\nIn this paper, we introduce EAGLE, an efficient framework\nfor speculative sampling. EAGLE conducts the drafting\nprocess auto-regressively at the more structured (second-\ntop-layer) feature level and mitigates sampling uncertainty\nin predicting the next feature by incorporating tokens from\none time step ahead. EAGLE is guaranteed to preserve the\noutput distribution of the LLM while significantly enhancing\ngeneration speed. On MT-bench, EAGLE is 3x faster than\nvanilla auto-regressive decoding, 2x faster than Lookahead,\nand 1.6x faster than Medusa.\nAcknowledgements.
